The chain rule therefore bounds the third directional derivative of the composed test by $$C_{z,\Phi}q_*^3\|\Delta\|^3.$$ This estimate holds at every intermediate real symmetric matrix, independently of eigenvalue gaps.

Taylor expansion to second order cancels the means and covariances in each replacement. Summing the third-order remainders gives total error $$C_{z,\Phi}q_*^6 r\theta^3
 =O\bigl(r^{-0.44+(3/2)\tau}\bigr)=o(1).$$ Finally $\sum_\alpha\beta_{\alpha\alpha}\mathcal W_\alpha$ has exactly the law $\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W$, proving the lemma. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-sw-lindeberg-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-sw-lindeberg</summary>

*Completion of Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">15</a>.* The coefficient bounds were proved after <a href="#eq:sw-finite-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-finite-matrix">[eq:sw-finite-matrix]</a>. That equation, Lemma <a href="#sw:trace-cut" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:trace-cut">18</a>, and Lemma <a href="#sw:lindeberg" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sw:lindeberg">19</a> prove the conditional comparison with $\mathcal S_r$ in the proposition. Each comparison concerns bounded tests, so its conditional error can be averaged by bounded convergence. By Proposition <a href="#prop:continuous-switching" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:continuous-switching">14</a>, the unconditional law of $\mathcal S_r$ is the law of $m$ for every $r$. This proves <a href="#eq:sw-trace-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-trace-limit">[eq:sw-trace-limit]</a>; bounded smooth tests suffice to determine finite-dimensional weak convergence.

We finish by explaining why these trace limits imply convergence of point measures, without assuming a uniform bound on their total number of points. Let $\nu^{(r)}$ be the eigenvalue counting measure of $H_r$, and define $$\sigma_r(\,\mathrm dx)=\frac{\nu^{(r)}(\,\mathrm dx)}{1+x^2}.$$ Its total mass is $\Im\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_r-i)^{-1}$, whose laws are tight by <a href="#eq:sw-trace-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sw-trace-limit">[eq:sw-trace-limit]</a>. Regarding $\sigma_r$ as a finite positive measure on the one-point compactification $\overline{\mathbb R}=\mathbb R\cup\{\infty\}$, its laws are therefore tight. For $z=e+i\eta\in\mathbb C_+$, the kernel $$k_z(x)=(1+x^2)\frac{\eta}{(x-e)^2+\eta^2}$$ extends continuously to $\overline{\mathbb R}$, with $k_z(\infty)=\eta$. Its integral against $\sigma_r$ is $\Im\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(H_r-z)^{-1}$. It follows that in any subsequential limit $\sigma$, the joint law of these integrals at a countable dense set of $z$’s is that of $\Im m(z)$. Continuity extends this conclusion to all $z$.

These integrals determine the de-weighted restriction of $\sigma$ to $\mathbb R$. Indeed, for $f\in C_c(\mathbb R)$, integrate the kernel against $\pi^{-1}f(e)\,\mathrm de$ and let $\eta\downarrow0$. On $\mathbb R$ the resulting kernel tends to $(1+x^2)f(x)$, by the Poisson approximate identity. The integrated kernels are bounded uniformly over $x\in\overline{\mathbb R}$ and $0<\eta\le1$: near the compact support this is the Poisson mass bound, and away from it the factor $1+x^2$ cancels the quadratic denominator. At infinity their value is $\eta\pi^{-1}\int f(e)\,\mathrm de$, which tends to zero. Dominated convergence is thus applicable to each finite measure $\sigma$, including any mass at infinity.

By <a href="#eq:marked-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-representation">[eq:marked-representation]</a>, applying the same inversion to $\Im m(z)$ gives $\int f\,\,\mathrm d\nu$. Taking a countable determining family of compactly supported tests, the joint distribution of these integrals is therefore exactly the law of $\nu$. The map from $\sigma$ to its de-weighted restriction is continuous for local vague convergence: for a compactly supported $f$, the function $(1+x^2)f(x)$, extended by zero at infinity, is continuous on $\overline{\mathbb R}$. Every subsequential local vague limit of $\nu^{(r)}$ consequently has the law of $\nu$. This proves the point-process assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:dbm"></a>
### Identification by finite-matrix Dyson Brownian motion

Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">15</a> approximates every microscopic subsequential law by the eigenvalues of a finite diagonal matrix plus GOE noise. We now apply fixed-energy universality to these finite matrices. Two details require attention: their limiting density must be normalized correctly, and the correlation-function conclusion must be converted into the Laplace-functional statement of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="pm-h-38"></a>
#### The fixed-energy input

<a id="eq:dbm-regularity"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-parameter-domain"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-free-convolution"></a>
For a real diagonal matrix $V=\mathop{\mathrm{diag}}(v_1,\ldots,v_m)$, write $$m_V(\zeta)=\frac1m\sum_{j=1}^m\frac1{v_j-\zeta}.$$ For positive parameters $g,G$, we call $V$ $(g,G)$-regular if, for fixed positive constants $c,C,C_V$, $$\label{eq:dbm-regularity}
 c\le \Im m_V(e+i\eta)\le C
 \quad (|e|\le G,\ g\le\eta\le10),
 \qquad \|V\|\le m^{C_V}.$$ The parameters may depend on $m$, and satisfy, for a fixed $\delta>0$, $$\label{eq:dbm-parameter-domain}
 m^{\delta-1}\le g\le m^{-\delta},
 \qquad G\le m^{-\delta}.$$ Let $W_m$ be standard normalized GOE, with entry variances $1/m$ off the diagonal and $2/m$ on the diagonal. The deterministic free-convolution comparison density associated with $V+\sqrt t\,W_m$ is described by the transform $$\label{eq:dbm-free-convolution}
 m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(\zeta)
   =m_V\bigl(\zeta+t\,m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(\zeta)\bigr),
 \qquad
 \rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(e)
   =\frac1\pi\lim_{\eta\downarrow0}
          \Im m_{\mathrm{fc},t}(e+i\eta).$$ Here the solution with positive imaginary part is used in the upper half-plane. See Biane (1997, Lemma 4, Proposition 2, and Corollary 2) <a id="pm-cite-33"></a>[[Biane](#ref-Biane)] for this analytic description of semicircular free convolution.

We state the specialization of the fixed-energy theorem that we need. Its density convention is important. If $p_H^{(m)}$ denotes the symmetrized joint eigenvalue density of a random matrix $H$, normalized to have integral one, then $$p_H^{(a)}(y_1,\ldots,y_a)
   =\int_{\mathbb R^{m-a}}p_H^{(m)}(y_1,\ldots,y_m)
        \,\mathrm dy_{a+1}\cdots\,\mathrm dy_m$$ also has integral one. It is not the factorial moment density.

<a id="stmt-thm-dbm-input"></a>
<div id="thm:dbm-input" class="theorem">

<a id="eq:dbm-time-window"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-marginal-test"></a>
**Theorem 20** (Fixed-energy DBM input). *Suppose a sequence of deterministic diagonal matrices $V$ of dimension $m$ satisfies <a href="#eq:dbm-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-regularity">[eq:dbm-regularity]</a> and <a href="#eq:dbm-parameter-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-parameter-domain">[eq:dbm-parameter-domain]</a>, with fixed constants. Fix $\sigma>0$, and suppose $$\label{eq:dbm-time-window}
 g m^\sigma\le t\le m^{-\sigma}G^2.$$ Assume also that $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(0)=1/\pi$. For every fixed positive integer $a$ and $O\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R^a)$, the difference $$\begin{aligned}
 \label{eq:dbm-marginal-test}
 &\int_{\mathbb R^a}O(\boldsymbol\alpha)
   p_{V+\sqrt t\,W_m}^{(a)}
      \left(\frac{\pi\alpha_1}{m},\ldots,
            \frac{\pi\alpha_a}{m}\right)
       \,\mathrm d\boldsymbol\alpha \notag\\
 &\hspace{12mm}-
 \int_{\mathbb R^a}O(\boldsymbol\alpha)
   p_{W_m}^{(a)}
      \left(\frac{\pi\alpha_1}{m},\ldots,
            \frac{\pi\alpha_a}{m}\right)
       \,\mathrm d\boldsymbol\alpha
 \longrightarrow0.
\end{aligned}$$ Consequently, the expected sums of $O$ over ordered tuples of distinct points of the two eigenvalue processes, both rescaled by $m/\pi$, differ by $o(1)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-dbm-input"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-dbm-input</summary>

*Proof.* The marginal statement is Theorem 2.2 of Landon, Sosoe, and Yau (2019) <a id="pm-cite-34"></a>[[LandonSosoeYau](#ref-LandonSosoeYau)], with Definition 2.1 and Equations (2.5)–(2.9) there, specialized to energy zero and equal densities $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t}(0)=\rho_{\mathrm{sc}}(0)=1/\pi$. The energy lies in the required interior window for any fixed interior fraction. That theorem gives a power-decaying bound for <a href="#eq:dbm-marginal-test" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-marginal-test">[eq:dbm-marginal-test]</a>. To pass to the last assertion, multiply each integral by $$\frac{(m)_a}{(m/\pi)^a},
 \qquad
 (m)_a=m(m-1)\cdots(m-a+1).$$ This is the Jacobian and ordered-tuple multiplicity for the unit-mass marginal convention, and it tends to $\pi^a$ for fixed $a$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-39"></a>
#### Regularity and the density adjustment

<a id="eq:dbm-raw-matrix"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-dimension"></a>
Fix a microscopic subsequential law $\nu$ supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:microscopic-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:microscopic-limit">13</a>. We use the parameters and conditional matrices of Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">15</a>: $$K=r^{1/100},\qquad T=r^\tau,\qquad
 0<\tau<\frac{\chi}{100000},\qquad
 I=\{j:|x_j|\le K\},\qquad q_*=|I|,$$ and $$\label{eq:dbm-raw-matrix}
 H_r=D_I+s_1I+\sqrt{s_2}\,\mathcal W_r,
 \qquad |s_1|\le C T,\qquad cT\le s_2\le CT.$$ Conditional on $\nu$, $\mathcal W_r$ is raw GOE of dimension $q_*$, with variances $1$ and $2$. All following almost-sure assertions concern the conditioning input $\nu$. In particular, <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a> gives $$\label{eq:dbm-dimension}
 q_*=2\rho K+O(K^{1-\chi}),
 \qquad
 T=q_*^{100\tau+o(1)}.$$ Thus $q_*>0$ eventually. We may decrease $\chi$ so that $\chi<1/4$.

<a id="eq:dbm-normalized-matrix"></a>
Put $\alpha_*=\pi\rho$. Scaling <a href="#eq:dbm-raw-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-raw-matrix">[eq:dbm-raw-matrix]</a> by $\alpha_*/q_*$ gives the normalized GOE-addition model $$\label{eq:dbm-normalized-matrix}
 \frac{\alpha_*}{q_*}H_r
   \ \stackrel{\mathrm{law}}{=}\
       V_*+\sqrt{t_*}\,W_{q_*},
 \qquad
 V_*=\frac{\alpha_*}{q_*}(D_I+s_1I),
 \qquad
 t_*=\frac{\alpha_*^2s_2}{q_*}.$$

<a id="stmt-dbm-regularity-density"></a>
<div id="dbm:regularity-density" class="lemma">

**Lemma 21**. *For almost every $\nu$, the matrices $V_*$ are eventually regular with $$g=\frac{T^{1/2}}{q_*},\qquad G=q_*^{-1/8}.$$ They satisfy the hypotheses <a href="#eq:dbm-parameter-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-parameter-domain">[eq:dbm-parameter-domain]</a> and <a href="#eq:dbm-time-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-time-window">[eq:dbm-time-window]</a> with $m=q_*$ and $\delta=\sigma=\tau$. Moreover, $$\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)\longrightarrow\frac1\pi.$$ If $c_r=\pi\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)$, then eventually $c_r>0$, and the data $c_rV_*$ and time $c_r^2t_*$ satisfy Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">20</a>, including its exact density condition.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-dbm-regularity-density"><summary>Proof of stmt-dbm-regularity-density</summary>

<a id="eq:dbm-original-domain"></a>
*Proof.* First, $\|V_*\|$ is bounded by <a href="#eq:dbm-dimension" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-dimension">[eq:dbm-dimension]</a>, since $T=o(K)$. We verify a slightly enlarged regularity domain, $$|\Re\zeta|\le2G,\qquad g/2\le\Im\zeta\le20,$$ so that subsequent dilation by $c_r=1+o(1)$ will be harmless. The normalized resolvent trace is $$m_{V_*}(\zeta)=
   \alpha_*^{-1}\sum_{j\in I}
        \frac1{x_j-(q_*\zeta/\alpha_*-s_1)}.$$ Writing $e=q_*\Re\zeta/\alpha_*-s_1$ and $u=q_*\Im\zeta/\alpha_*$, the enlarged domain has $$\label{eq:dbm-original-domain}
 |e|=O(K^{7/8}+T)=o(K),\qquad
 cT^{1/2}\le u\le CK.$$ The lower height exceeds a fixed positive power of $K$. The uniform complex resolvent estimate controls the Poisson sum down to heights of order $T^{1/2}$, while the count discrepancy controls truncation at $K$. Equation <a href="#eq:mesoscopic-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mesoscopic-limit">[eq:mesoscopic-limit]</a>, with a radius comparable to $K$ and any fixed exponent smaller than $50\tau$, therefore gives $$\sum_j\frac{u}{(x_j-e)^2+u^2}
       =\pi\rho+o(1)$$ uniformly in <a href="#eq:dbm-original-domain" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-original-domain">[eq:dbm-original-domain]</a>; here we used the imaginary part of <a href="#eq:marked-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-representation">[eq:marked-representation]</a>. For $u/K$ sufficiently small, the omitted terms with $|x_j|>K$ sum to $O(u/K)$, uniformly for $|e|=o(K)$, by the linear count bound following from <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a>. Hence the retained sum has a positive lower bound. For $u/K$ bounded below, every retained point has $|x_j-e|\le2K$ eventually, so $$\sum_{j\in I}\frac{u}{(x_j-e)^2+u^2}
   \ge \frac{q_*u}{4K^2+u^2}\ge c>0.$$ Its upper bound follows by positivity from the full sum. This proves regularity on the enlarged domain.

With $m=q_*$, the relevant powers are $$g=m^{-1+50\tau+o(1)},\qquad
 t_*=m^{-1+100\tau+o(1)},\qquad G=m^{-1/8}.$$ In particular, $$\frac{t_*}{g m^\tau}=m^{49\tau+o(1)}\longrightarrow\infty,
 \qquad
 \frac{t_*}{m^{-\tau}G^2}
     =m^{-3/4+101\tau+o(1)}\longrightarrow0.$$ The inequalities $m^{\tau-1}\le g\le m^{-\tau}$ and $G\le m^{-\tau}$ follow as well. Our bound on $\tau$ makes all these exponent inequalities strict.

<a id="eq:dbm-boundary-equation"></a>
It remains to determine the free-convolution density. At $\zeta=0$, Equation <a href="#eq:dbm-free-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-free-convolution">[eq:dbm-free-convolution]</a> becomes $$\label{eq:dbm-boundary-equation}
 w=F_r(w),\qquad
 F_r(w)=\alpha_*^{-1}\sum_{j\in I}
     \frac1{x_j-(\alpha_*s_2w-s_1)}.$$ For $|w-i|\le1/4$, the argument $z=\alpha_*s_2w-s_1$ has $\Im z\asymp T$ and $|z|=O(T)$. Equations <a href="#eq:mesoscopic-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mesoscopic-limit">[eq:mesoscopic-limit]</a> and <a href="#eq:marked-representation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:marked-representation">[eq:marked-representation]</a> imply $$\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_j\frac1{x_j-z}=i\pi\rho+o(1)$$ uniformly in this disk. The real constant $m_{\rm r}$ has been subtracted in this formula. Furthermore, $$\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac1{x_j-z}
   =\mathop{\mathrm{PV}}\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac1{x_j}
      +\sum_{|x_j|>K}\frac{z}{x_j(x_j-z)}
   =O(K^{-\chi}+T/K).$$ The first bound is partial summation of <a href="#eq:discrepancy" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:discrepancy">[eq:discrepancy]</a>; the second uses its linear count bound and $|z|=o(K)$. Thus $F_r(w)=i+o(1)$ uniformly on $|w-i|\le1/4$.

<a id="eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity"></a>
Rouché’s theorem applied to $w-F_r(w)$ and $w-i$ shows that <a href="#eq:dbm-boundary-equation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-boundary-equation">[eq:dbm-boundary-equation]</a> has exactly one zero, counted with multiplicity, in this disk for all large $r$. The zero $w_r$ is therefore simple, and $w_r\to i$. The implicit function theorem continues it to a solution near $\zeta=0$ of the full fixed-point equation, still with positive imaginary part. This solution is the physical branch. Indeed, every upper-half-plane solution $w$, at $\Im\zeta>0$, satisfies $$\label{eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity}
 \frac1{q_*}\sum_{j\in I}
   |(V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*w|^{-2}
       =\frac{\Im w}{\Im\zeta+t_*\Im w}<\frac1{t_*}.$$ If two distinct such solutions existed, subtracting their equations and dividing by their difference would give $$1=\frac{t_*}{q_*}\sum_{j\in I}
  \frac1{((V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*w_1)
          ((V_*)_{jj}-\zeta-t_*w_2)}.$$ Cauchy–Schwarz and <a href="#eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity">[eq:dbm-uniqueness-identity]</a> make the absolute value of the right-hand side strictly less than one. This contradiction proves uniqueness and identifies the analytic continuation. Its boundary value gives $\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)=\Im w_r/\pi\to1/\pi$.

Finally, simultaneous replacement of $V_*,t_*$ by $c_rV_*,c_r^2t_*$ dilates the free-convolution law by $c_r$. For example its transform is $c_r^{-1}m_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(\zeta/c_r)$, as follows directly from <a href="#eq:dbm-free-convolution" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-free-convolution">[eq:dbm-free-convolution]</a>. Its density at zero is exactly $$c_r^{-1}\rho_{\mathrm{fc},t_*}(0)=\frac1\pi.$$ Since $c_r\to1$, the enlarged regularity domain proves <a href="#eq:dbm-regularity" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-regularity">[eq:dbm-regularity]</a> for $c_rV_*$ with the original $g,G$. The strict power margins preserve <a href="#eq:dbm-time-window" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-time-window">[eq:dbm-time-window]</a> for $c_r^2t_*$, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

The conclusion is conditional on almost every $\nu$, which is enough for applying Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">20</a> to a deterministic sequence. No assertion of switching invariance conditional on $\nu$ is used. The rescaled eigenvalues supplied by that theorem are, by <a href="#eq:dbm-normalized-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-normalized-matrix">[eq:dbm-normalized-matrix]</a>, precisely <a id="eq:dbm-final-coordinates"></a>$$\label{eq:dbm-final-coordinates}
 \frac{q_*}{\pi}\frac{c_r\alpha_*}{q_*}
       \lambda_j(H_r)
       =c_r\rho\,\lambda_j(H_r).$$ Define $c_r=1$ at any exceptional initial values where the preceding construction is not defined; these values have no effect on the limit.

<a id="pm-h-40"></a>
#### The GOE limit and uniform count moments

Let $$\mathcal G_m=\sum_{j=1}^m
       \delta_{(m/\pi)\lambda_j(W_m)}.$$ These point processes converge vaguely in law, along all dimensions, to a common locally finite point process, which we denote by $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}$. To check normalization directly, the eigenvalues of $\sqrt m\,W_m$ have joint density proportional to $$\exp\left(-\frac14\sum_j y_j^2\right)
       \prod_{i<j}|y_i-y_j|.$$ This follows from orthogonal diagonalization of the matrix density $\exp(-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}H^2/4)$; the change in an off-diagonal coordinate under an infinitesimal basis rotation is the corresponding eigenvalue difference, yielding the Vandermonde Jacobian. Theorem 1 of Valkó and Virág (2009) <a id="pm-cite-35"></a>[[ValkoVirag](#ref-ValkoVirag)], for $\beta=1$ and center zero, gives vague convergence after multiplying these eigenvalues by $2\sqrt m$. Its bulk condition $m^{1/6}(2\sqrt m-0)\to\infty$ holds. Dividing the resulting coordinates by $2\pi$ gives $\mathcal G_m$, as claimed.

The DBM input compares each fixed correlation order. To recover Laplace functionals, we will bracket them by finite alternating sums of those correlation observables. The following exponential bound on the reference GOE counts will make the gap between successive brackets tend to zero, uniformly in the matrix dimension.

<a id="stmt-lem-goe-counts"></a>
<div id="lem:goe-counts" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22**. *For every compact interval $J\subset\mathbb R$ and every $b>0$, $$\sup_{m\ge1}\mathbb E\exp\bigl(b\,\mathcal G_m(J)\bigr)<\infty.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-goe-counts"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-goe-counts</summary>

*Proof.* We bound the GOE count by twice a Gaussian unitary count plus one, using the Gaussian decimation identity of Forrester and Rains (2001, arXiv version 2, Theorem 5.2 and Equation (5.9)) <a id="pm-cite-36"></a>[[ForresterRains](#ref-ForresterRains)]. We derive that identity in our normalization, then use the unitary ensemble’s projection kernel to bound its count moments.

<a id="pm-h-41"></a>
###### Gaussian decimation.

Write $\mathrm O_m$ for the decreasingly ordered points with density proportional to $$\Delta(y)\prod_{i=1}^m b_0(y_i),\qquad
 b_0(y)=e^{-y^2/2},\qquad
 \Delta(y)=\prod_{i<j}(y_i-y_j).$$ They have the law of the eigenvalues of $\sqrt m\,W_m/\sqrt2$. Thus the count $\mathcal G_m(J)$ is the $\mathrm O_m$ count in $$J_m=\frac{\pi}{\sqrt{2m}}J,
 \qquad |J_m|=O(m^{-1/2}).$$

<a id="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial"></a>
Superpose independent $\mathrm O_m$ and $\mathrm O_{m+1}$, and order the resulting points as $z_1>\cdots>z_{2m+1}$. Apart from a constant and the common Gaussian factor, their density is the polynomial $$P(z)=\sum_{\substack{S\subset\{1,\ldots,2m+1\}\\|S|=m}}
          \Delta(z_S)\Delta(z_{S^c}),$$ where each sublist is in the inherited order. The parity–Vandermonde factorization of Forrester and Rains (2001, arXiv version 2, Lemma 4.1 and Equation (4.2)) <a id="pm-cite-37"></a>[[ForresterRains](#ref-ForresterRains)] is $$\label{eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial}
 P(z)=c_m\Delta(z_1,z_3,\ldots,z_{2m+1})
             \Delta(z_2,z_4,\ldots,z_{2m}),\qquad c_m>0.$$ If $z_i=z_j$ with $i,j$ of the same parity, terms having both indices in one sublist vanish. The other terms cancel in pairs under exchanging membership of $i,j$: the sign ratio is $(-1)^{j-i-1}=-1$, since each intervening index belongs to exactly one sublist. Hence every factor on the right divides $P$. Both sides have degree $m^2$, so the quotient is constant. It is positive on strictly decreasing $z$, proving <a href="#eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial">[eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial]</a>.

<a id="eq:dbm-even-density"></a>
Fix the even points $y_i=z_{2i}$. The odd points interlace them, one in each of $$(y_1,\infty),\ (y_2,y_1),\ldots,(y_m,y_{m-1}),\
 (-\infty,y_m).$$ To integrate their Vandermonde times their Gaussian factors, use the monic polynomials $$p_j(x)=(-1)^j\frac{b_0^{(j)}(x)}{b_0(x)},
 \qquad 0\le j\le m.$$ Writing the Vandermonde as the determinant of these polynomials reduces the integral, by multilinearity, to a determinant of integrals over the displayed intervals. Replace each row by the sum of it and all preceding rows. For column $j\ge1$, the first $m$ rows become $$\int_{y_i}^\infty p_j(x)b_0(x)\,\mathrm dx
       =p_{j-1}(y_i)b_0(y_i),$$ and the last row is zero. In column zero the last row is $\int_\mathbb Rb_0$. Expansion along that last row leaves a Vandermonde in the $y_i$ times $\prod_i b_0(y_i)$. More explicitly, the ascending-degree polynomial columns give signs $(-1)^{m(m+1)/2}$ for the odd-point Vandermonde and $(-1)^{m(m-1)/2}$ for the even-point Vandermonde, while expansion along the last row contributes $(-1)^m$. Their product is one. Thus, if $x_0>y_1>x_1>\cdots>y_m>x_m$, the integral just evaluated is exactly $$\int_{\text{interlacing}}
        \Delta(x_0,\ldots,x_m)\prod_{i=0}^m b_0(x_i)
                \,\mathrm dx_0\cdots\,\mathrm dx_m
     =\sqrt{2\pi}\,\Delta(y)\prod_{i=1}^m b_0(y_i).$$ Together with the even-point factors already present in <a href="#eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial">[eq:dbm-decimation-polynomial]</a>, this proves that the even points have density proportional to $$\label{eq:dbm-even-density}
 \Delta(y)^2\prod_{i=1}^m e^{-y_i^2}.$$

<a id="pm-h-42"></a>
###### Projection kernel and count moments.

The even-point density now gives a direct bound on count moments. Let $\psi_j(x)$ be the degree-$j$ orthonormal polynomial for the weight $e^{-x^2}$, multiplied by $e^{-x^2/2}$. The kernel $$K_m(x,y)=\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}\psi_j(x)\psi_j(y)$$ is the rank-$m$ orthogonal projection in $L^2(\mathbb R)$ onto their span. The symmetric density in <a href="#eq:dbm-even-density" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-even-density">[eq:dbm-even-density]</a> is $(m!)^{-1}\det(K_m(y_i,y_j))_{i,j=1}^m$. Indeed the determinant is a squared weighted Vandermonde, and orthonormality gives its integral $m!$. More generally its order-$a$ factorial moment density is $\det(K_m(y_i,y_j))_{i,j=1}^a$. For completeness, expand a size-$a$ determinant by Cauchy–Binet as the sum of squared minors indexed by $a$ basis functions. Integrating its last variable uses their orthogonality, and each minor of size $a-1$ occurs $m-a+1$ times. Iterating this identity proves the factorial density formula with its stated normalization.

We need a bound uniform in both $m$ and location. The operator $-\partial_x^2+x^2$ has eigenvalue $2j+1$ on $\psi_j$. This follows by conjugating it by $e^{-x^2/2}$: on the polynomial factor it acts as $-p''+2xp'+p$, preserves successive degree spaces, is symmetric for the Gaussian inner product, and has leading coefficient multiplier $2j+1$. Consequently every $f$ in the range of $K_m$ satisfies $$\|f'\|_2^2\le(2m-1)\|f\|_2^2.$$ The elementary bound $|f(x)|^2\le2\|f'\|_2\|f\|_2$, obtained by integrating the derivative of $|f|^2$, gives $$K_m(x,x)
   =\sup_{\substack{f\in\operatorname{Ran}K_m\\\|f\|_2=1}}
       |f(x)|^2
   \le2\sqrt{2m}.$$ If $N_m^{\mathrm{even}}$ is the number of even points in $J_m$, the Gram determinant inequality yields, for every $a\ge0$, $$\mathbb E(N_m^{\mathrm{even}})_a
   \le \bigl(2\sqrt{2m}\,|J_m|\bigr)^a\le C_J^a.$$ For $a>m$ the left side is zero. Hence, for every $v>0$, $$\mathbb Ee^{vN_m^{\mathrm{even}}}
   =\sum_{a=0}^m
       \frac{(e^v-1)^a}{a!}\mathbb E(N_m^{\mathrm{even}})_a
   \le\exp\bigl(C_J(e^v-1)\bigr).$$ In any interval the total superposition count is at most twice its even count plus one. The original $\mathrm O_m$ count is no greater than the superposition count. Thus $$\mathbb Ee^{b\mathcal G_m(J)}
   \le e^b\,\mathbb Ee^{2bN_m^{\mathrm{even}}},$$ which proves the assertion. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-43"></a>
#### From correlation functions to Laplace functionals

<a id="eq:dbm-insertion-limit"></a>
Let $\nu^{(r)}=\sum_{j=1}^{q_*}\delta_{\lambda_j(H_r)}$ denote the raw eigenvalue measure in <a href="#eq:dbm-raw-matrix" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-raw-matrix">[eq:dbm-raw-matrix]</a>. Proposition <a href="#prop:gaussian-insertion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:gaussian-insertion">15</a> asserts $$\label{eq:dbm-insertion-limit}
 \nu^{(r)}\ \Longrightarrow\ \nu$$ in local vague topology, unconditionally. We will identify the law of $\rho$-dilated $\nu$ by first applying DBM conditional on $\nu$.

<a id="eq:dbm-bonferroni"></a>
Fix a nonnegative $h\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)$, and put $f=1-e^{-h}$, so $0\le f\le1$. For a finite point measure $\zeta=\sum_i\delta_{y_i}$, define $$A_0(\zeta)=1,\qquad
 A_a(\zeta)=\frac1{a!}
    \sum_{i_1,\ldots,i_a\ {\rm distinct}}
        \prod_{j=1}^a f(y_{i_j})\quad(a\ge1),
 \qquad
 B_\ell(\zeta)=\sum_{a=0}^{\ell}(-1)^aA_a(\zeta).$$ The finite inclusion-exclusion inequalities give $$\label{eq:dbm-bonferroni}
 B_{2\ell+1}(\zeta)
   \le \exp\left(-\int h\,\mathrm d\zeta\right)
   \le B_{2\ell}(\zeta).$$ One can see these inequalities by taking independent Bernoulli variables of success probabilities $f(y_i)$: the middle quantity is their probability of no successes, and the displayed sums are the Bonferroni bounds for that event.

<a id="eq:dbm-bracket-width"></a>
<a id="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace"></a>
Conditional on almost every $\nu$, let $\zeta_r$ have the point coordinates $c_r\rho\,\lambda_j(H_r)$. Lemma <a href="#dbm:regularity-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dbm:regularity-density">21</a> and Theorem <a href="#thm:dbm-input" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:dbm-input">20</a>, applied with the test $\prod_{j=1}^a f(\alpha_j)$, imply for each fixed $\ell$ that $$\mathbb E[B_\ell(\zeta_r)\mid\nu]
       -\mathbb EB_\ell(\mathcal G_{q_*})\longrightarrow0.$$ The expectation in the second term is over a reference GOE matrix of the now deterministic conditional dimension $q_*$. To close the brackets uniformly in that dimension, choose a compact interval $J$ containing $\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}h$. Then $$A_a(\mathcal G_m)\le
     \binom{\mathcal G_m(J)}a
     \le 2^{-a}3^{\mathcal G_m(J)}.$$ The last inequality follows from $\sum_{a\ge0}2^a\binom{N}{a}=3^N$. Lemma <a href="#lem:goe-counts" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:goe-counts">22</a> consequently gives $$\label{eq:dbm-bracket-width}
 \sup_m\mathbb E\bigl[B_{2\ell}(\mathcal G_m)
                 -B_{2\ell+1}(\mathcal G_m)\bigr]
       \le C_J2^{-(2\ell+1)}.$$ Using <a href="#eq:dbm-bonferroni" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-bonferroni">[eq:dbm-bonferroni]</a> for both models, first sending $r\to\infty$ at fixed $\ell$, and then sending $\ell\to\infty$, proves $$\label{eq:dbm-conditional-laplace}
 \mathbb E\left[\exp\left(-\int h\,\mathrm d\zeta_r\right)\,\middle|\,\nu\right]
   \longrightarrow
 \mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,\mathrm d\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right).$$ Here we also used the vague GOE limit and $q_*\to\infty$. Only the reference count moments were needed in <a href="#eq:dbm-bracket-width" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-bracket-width">[eq:dbm-bracket-width]</a>; no uniform moment bound for the deformed model has been assumed. Since both sides of the conditional Laplace comparison lie in $[0,1]$, bounded convergence now removes the conditioning in <a href="#eq:dbm-conditional-laplace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace">[eq:dbm-conditional-laplace]</a>.

<a id="eq:dbm-limit-identification"></a>
For $c>0$, let $\mathcal D_c\zeta$ denote the point measure obtained by multiplying all positions of $\zeta$ by $c$. The map $(c,\zeta)\mapsto\mathcal D_c\zeta$ is continuous when $c$ tends to a positive number and $\zeta$ converges locally vaguely. Indeed, the inverse images of a fixed compact support remain in a common compact interval, and the corresponding test functions converge uniformly there; vague convergence also bounds the masses on a slightly larger compact interval. By Lemma <a href="#dbm:regularity-density" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="dbm:regularity-density">21</a>, $c_r\to1$ almost surely. Combining this with <a href="#eq:dbm-insertion-limit" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-insertion-limit">[eq:dbm-insertion-limit]</a> and the usual joint convergence with a constant yields $$\zeta_r=\mathcal D_{c_r\rho}\nu^{(r)}
       \ \Longrightarrow\ \mathcal D_\rho\nu.$$ The Laplace functional of a compactly supported continuous nonnegative test is bounded and continuous in local vague topology. Thus <a href="#eq:dbm-conditional-laplace" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-conditional-laplace">[eq:dbm-conditional-laplace]</a>, after averaging, identifies $$\label{eq:dbm-limit-identification}
 \mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,\mathrm d\mathcal D_\rho\nu\right)
   =\mathbb E\exp\left(-\int h\,\mathrm d\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}\right)
 \qquad (0\le h\in C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)).$$ For any $0\le h\in C_c(\mathbb R)$, convolution with a nonnegative smooth approximate identity gives nonnegative smooth functions converging uniformly to $h$, all supported in one fixed compact interval. Both limiting point measures have finitely many points there almost surely. The corresponding Laplace variables converge pointwise and are bounded by one, so bounded convergence extends <a href="#eq:dbm-limit-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-limit-identification">[eq:dbm-limit-identification]</a> to every such $h$.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-goe-counts-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-goe-counts</summary>

*Proof of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.* Start with any sequence of admissible graph sizes tending to infinity. Proposition <a href="#prop:microscopic-limit" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:microscopic-limit">13</a> supplies a further subsequence on which the unscaled microscopic measures $\nu_n$ converge in law to a process $\nu$ of the form used above. The graph point measure in the theorem is $\mathcal D_\rho\nu_n$. Bounded continuity of its Laplace functional and <a href="#eq:dbm-limit-identification" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:dbm-limit-identification">[eq:dbm-limit-identification]</a> show that its Laplace expectation, along this further subsequence, tends to the Laplace expectation of $\mathcal S_{\mathrm{GOE}}$. The reference GOE expectations at dimension $n$ tend to the same value, along all sizes. Every original subsequence therefore has a further subsequence along which their difference tends to zero, which proves the required full-sequence limit. All spectral estimates and negligible exclusions used to construct the subsequential law were events in the original simple-graph model; no conditioning on connectedness, bipartiteness, or any additional graph property enters the conclusion. ◻

</details>

  "figures": [
    {
      "caption": "A two-edge switch on an induced matching. The marked pairs $(v_{1},w_{1})$ and $(v_{2},w_{2})$ are replaced by $(v_{1},w_{2})$ and $(v_{2},w_{1})$. Each endpoint loses one edge and gains one edge, so its degree is preserved. All other graph edges remain in place, and the marked list records the new pairs. The same exchange reverses the operation.",
      "figure_id": "fig:two-edge-switch",
      "pdf_page": 19,
      "source_evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "fig:two-edge-switch",
          "exact_quote": "A two-edge switch on an induced matching. The marked pairs\n \\((v_1,w_1)\\) and \\((v_2,w_2)\\) are replaced by\n \\((v_1,w_2)\\) and \\((v_2,w_1)\\). Each endpoint loses one edge and gains\n one edge, so its degree is preserved. All other graph edge",
          "source_object": "fig:0001"
        }
      ],
      "source_kind": "tikz_pgf",
      "tikz_pgf_source": "\\begin{tikzpicture}[x=1cm,y=1cm,\n vertex/.style={circle,fill=black,inner sep=2pt},\n edge/.style={line width=.8pt},every node/.style={font=\\small}]\n \\foreach \\offset/\\tag in {0/a,5.1/b}{\n   \\node[vertex,label=left:{$v_1$}] (\\tag v1) at (\\offset,1.6) {};\n   \\node[vertex,label=left:{$v_2$}] (\\tag v2) at (\\offset,0) {};\n   \\node[vertex,label=right:{$w_1$}] (\\tag w1) at (\\offset+2,1.6) {};\n   \\node[vertex,label=right:{$w_2$}] (\\tag w2) at (\\offset+2,0) {};\n }\n \\draw[edge] (av1)--(aw1) (av2)--(aw2);\n \\draw[edge] (bv1)--(bw2) (bv2)--(bw1);\n \\draw[-{Stealth[length=2.5mm]},line width=.7pt] (2.6,.8)--(4.5,.8)\n    node[midway,above] {switch};\n \\node at (1,-.65) {original pairs};\n \\node at (6.1,-.65) {new pairs};\n\\end{tikzpicture}",
      "witness_description": "Two graph diagrams showing an edge switch operation on four vertices. On the left, labeled \"original pairs\", four vertices $v_1$ (top-left), $v_2$ (bottom-left), $w_1$ (top-right), and $w_2$ (bottom-right) are connected by two horizontal parallel edges: $(v_1, w_1)$ and $(v_2, w_2)$. In the center, a horizontal arrow pointing right is labeled \"switch\" above it. On the right, labeled \"new pairs\", the same four vertices $v_1, v_2, w_1, w_2$ are connected by two crossing diagonal edges: $(v_1, w_2)$ and $(v_2, w_1)$."
    }
  ],

      "source_pm_id": "PM-219.2",
      "source_quote": "For every fixed integer $d\\ge3$",

      "target_pm_id": "PM-219.1",

      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
    {
      "basis": "inferred",
      "direction": "symmetric",
      "explanation": "Both papers establish bulk universality on random regular graphs by showing asymptotic convergence of local eigenvalue point processes to the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble using test functions.",
      "relation": "shared_technique",
      "source_anchor": "stmt-thm-main",
      "source_pm_id": "PM-219.2",
      "source_quote": "every nonnegative continuous compactly supported",
      "target_anchor": "thm:main",
      "target_pm_id": "PM-219.1",
      "target_quote": "nonnegative $f\\in C_c(\\mathbb R)$",
      "verification": "PENDING"
    },
